\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. See for example
[
FZ99
, Lemma 2.8]
. Recall that, if
\beta\in\Phi
and
n
is a positive integer, then the
divided power

X_{\beta}^{(n)}
denotes
\frac{X_{\beta}^{n}}{n!}
, which is an element of the envelopping algebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

Let
V
be a representation of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{\alpha}
and
v^{-}\in V
(resp.
v^{+}\in V
) a lowest (resp. highest) weight vector of weight
-m
(resp.
m
) for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

X_{\alpha}^{(n)}v^{-}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\quad n>m\\
\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}v^{-}&\text{if}\quad n=m.\end{cases}\quad X_{-\alpha}^{(n)}v^{+}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\quad n>m\\
\overline{s}_{\alpha}v^{+}&\text{if}\quad n=m.\end{cases}

Next, we do some calculations that will be crucial in the following.

Lemma 5.4.3
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{C}
,
g\in G
and
N=\langle-w\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
. Then

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(gx_{\beta}(t))=\begin{aligned} \begin{cases}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(g)&\text{if}\quad w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\\
\sum_{n=0}^{N}t^{n}Q_{N-n}(g)&\text{if}\quad w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\end{cases}\end{aligned}

for some
Q_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[G]
, such that
Q_{0}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,s_{\beta}w}
and
Q_{N}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
.

Proof.

If
w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}
, the statement follows from Lemma
5.2.1
. Otherwise, we have that

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(gx_{\beta}(t))=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}\overline{v}\varphi_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{-}(gX_{\beta}^{(n)}\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+}).

If
w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}
, then
\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+}
is a lowest weight vector for
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{\beta}
of weight
\langle w\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
, hence Lemma
5.4.2
applies. In particular, for
n>N
the corresponding factor of the above sum is zero. For
n\in\{0,\dots,N\}
we set
Q_{N-n}(g)=\overline{v}\varphi_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{-}(gX_{\beta}^{(n)}\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+})
. Clearly,
Q_{N}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
. Moreover,
X_{\beta}^{(N)}\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+}=\overline{s}_{\beta}{{}^{-1}}\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+}.
But since
w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}
, then
\overline{w}=\overline{s}_{\beta}\overline{s_{\beta}w}
. The lemma follows.
∎

We have a variation of the previous lemma relative to the action of elements of
U^{-}
.

Lemma 5.4.4
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{C}
,
g\in G
and
N=\langle w\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
. Then

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(gx_{-\beta}(t))=\begin{aligned} \begin{cases}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(g)&\text{if}\quad w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\\
\sum_{n=0}^{N}t^{n}Q_{N-n}(g)&\text{if}\quad w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\end{cases}\end{aligned}

for some
Q_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[G]
, such that
Q_{0}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,s_{\beta}w}
and
Q_{N}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
.

Proof.

Literally the same proof of Lemma
5.4.3
. Just notice that if
w{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}
, then
\overline{w}v_{\varpi_{\alpha}}^{+}
is a highest weight vector for
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{\beta}
of weight
\langle w\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle.

∎

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{C}
,
g\in G
and
N=\langle v\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
. Then

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(x_{\beta}(t)g)=\begin{aligned} \begin{cases}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(g)&\text{if}\quad v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\\
\sum_{n=0}^{N}t^{n}P_{N-n}(g)&\text{if}\quad v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\end{cases}\end{aligned}

for some
P_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[G]
, such that
P_{0}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\beta}v,w}
and
P_{N}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
.

Proof.

One can prove the statement by direct calculation, otherwise we can reduce to Lemma
5.4.3
. Following
[
FZ99
]
, let
(-)^{\iota}
be the involutive anti-authomorphism of the group
G
defined by

x_{\gamma}(t)^{\iota}=x_{\gamma}(t)\quad x_{-\gamma}(t)^{\iota}=x_{-\gamma}(t)\quad h^{\iota}=h{{}^{-1}}\quad\text{for}\quad\gamma\in\Delta,\,\,h\in T.

By
[
FZ99
, Proposition 2.7]
, we have that for any
\gamma\in\Delta
and
g\in G

\Delta^{\varpi_{\gamma}}_{v,w}(g)=\Delta^{\varpi_{\gamma}^{*}}_{ww_{0},vw_{0}}(g^{\iota})

(44)

In particular, form Lemma
5.4.3
we deduce that

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(x_{\beta}(t)g)=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}^{*}}_{ww_{0},vw_{0}}(g^{\iota}x_{\beta}(t))=\begin{aligned} \begin{cases}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}^{*}}_{ww_{0},vw_{0}}(g^{\iota})=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(g)&\text{if}\quad w_{0}v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\\
\sum_{n=0}^{M}t^{n}Q_{M-n}(g^{\iota})&\text{if}\quad w_{0}v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\end{cases}\end{aligned}

where
M=\langle-vw_{0}\varpi_{\alpha}^{*},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
. Since
\varpi_{\alpha}^{*}=-w_{0}\varpi_{\alpha}
we have that
M=N
. Then we set
P_{n}(g)=Q_{n}(g^{\iota})
. Using again
[
FZ99
]
[Proposition 2.7], we deduce the desired expression for
P_{0}
and
P_{N}
from the expression of
Q_{0}
and
Q_{N}
given in Lemma
5.4.3
. Finally, notice that

w_{0}v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{+}{\,\Longleftrightarrow\,}v{{}^{-1}}\beta\in\Phi^{-}.

∎

Remark 5.4.6
.

If
\langle v\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle=0
, then
s_{\beta}v\varpi_{\alpha}=v\varpi_{\alpha}-\langle v\varpi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle\beta=v\varpi_{\alpha}
. Hence
P_{0}
and
P_{N}
agree in this case because of Lemma
5.2.1
, so there’s no ambiguity. For
Q_{0}
and
Q_{N}
we have the same phenomenon. The convention about the enumeration of the coefficients
P
is due to the fact that we want it to be compatible with the expansion of
t^{N}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}(x_{\beta}(t{{}^{-1}})g)
. The same applies to the coefficients
Q
.

The next statement is a special case of the previous lemma.

Lemma 5.4.7
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{C}
and
g\in G
. We have that

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,w}(x_{\beta}(t)g)=\begin{aligned} \begin{cases}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,w}(g)&\text{if}\quad\alpha\neq\beta\\
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,w}(g)+t\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},w}(g)&\text{if}\quad\alpha=\beta.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

Proof.

Obvious from Lemma
5.4.5
.
∎

Remark 5.4.8

(A remark on products)
.

We fix a quite obvious convention relative to products. Suppose that two complex, semisimple, simply connected groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are given. Moreover, suppose that, for
i=1,2
and
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta_{i}
, the data of
T_{i}
,
B_{i}\subseteq G_{i}

and of an
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-triple
(X_{\alpha_{i}},H_{\alpha_{i}},X_{-\alpha_{i}})
are also given. Then, we automatically make the following choices in
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}
: the torus
T=T_{1}\times T_{2}
, the Borel
B=B_{1}\times B_{2}
. Moreover, for
\alpha\in\Delta=\Delta_{1}\sqcup\Delta_{2}
, the
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
triple associated to
\alpha
, in
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, is the one in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1/2}
accordingly if
\alpha\in\Delta_{1/2}
.

With this choice, if
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\in\Delta_{1}
, then
\overline{s_{\alpha}}=(\overline{s_{\alpha_{1}}},e)
. If
\alpha=\alpha_{2}\in\Delta_{2}
, then
\overline{s_{\alpha}}=(e,\overline{s_{\alpha_{2}}})
. Hence, for any
w=(w_{1},w_{2})\in W=W_{1}\times W_{2}
, we have that

\overline{w}=(\overline{w_{1}},\overline{w_{2}}).

The fundamental weights of
T_{1}\times T_{2}
are of the form
(\varpi_{\alpha_{1}},0)
and
(0,\varpi_{\alpha_{2}})
and we have

\Delta^{(\varpi_{\alpha_{1}},0)}_{(v_{1},v_{2}),(w_{1},w_{2})}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha_{1}}}_{v_{1},w_{1}}\otimes 1\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta^{(0,\varpi_{\alpha_{2}})}_{(v_{1},v_{2}),(w_{1},w_{2})}=1\otimes\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha_{2}}}_{v_{2},w_{2}}.

(45)

5.5
Technical properties of minors II: algebraic independence

We study algebraic independence of, the restriction, of some families of generalised minors to some
T
-stable subgroup of
U
.

Fix
z\in W
and
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
. Let
l=\ell(z)
and
t=t_{\mathbf{i}}
. For
1\leq j\leq l
,
x_{j}\in\mathbb{C}[U(z)]
denotes the
j
-th cluster variable of the seed
t
. We refer to Section
5.3.3
for the definitions and the notation of this section.

Left twist: the case of U(z)

We fix
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z)
. In this subsection, for
\beta\in\Delta
and
v,w\in W
, we denote by
D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
the restriction of
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
to
U(z)
.

Lemma 5.5.1
.

The following equality holds

\displaystyle D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq\alpha^{\min}}^{-1}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\alpha^{\min}-1}(D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}})^{c_{j}}

for some non-negative integers
c_{j}
. Moreover, for
j<\alpha^{\min}
, we have that
D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}=D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{e,z_{\leq j}^{-1}}
.

Proof.

Note that
z_{\leq\alpha^{min}}^{-1}=z^{\prime}s_{\alpha}
where
z^{\prime}=s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{(i_{\alpha^{\min}-1})}
, or
z^{\prime}=e
if
\alpha^{\min}=1
. We apply Theorem
5.2.2
with
w=z^{\prime}
and
v=e
.

Note that
s_{\alpha}\not\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z^{\prime})
, hence
z^{\prime}\varpi_{\alpha}=\varpi_{\alpha}
and so
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,z^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}
by Lemma
5.2.1
. Moreover,
D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}=1
. Similarly, from Lemma
5.4.1
we deduce that
D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}}=D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},e}=0
. So, Theorem
5.2.2
specialises to the following identity:

\displaystyle D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq\alpha^{min}}^{-1}}=\prod_{\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}}(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z^{\prime}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}.

But for
\beta\neq\alpha
,
(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z^{\prime}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}=(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}
because of Lemma
5.2.1
. Moreover, if
s_{\beta}\notin{\operatorname{supp}}(z^{\prime})
,
(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}=1
.
If
s_{\beta}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z^{\prime})
and

k_{\beta}=\max\{j\in\{1,\dots,\alpha^{\min}-1\}\,:\,i_{j}=\beta\},

by Lemma
5.2.1
we have that

(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}=(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k_{\beta}}^{-1}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}.

In particular, for
j<\alpha^{\min}
, setting

c_{j}=\begin{cases}-a_{\beta,\alpha}&\text{if}\quad j=k_{\beta}\quad\text{for some}\quad\beta\neq\alpha.\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

we have proved the first part of the statement. The last part is obvious from Lemma
5.2.1
and the definition of
\alpha^{\min}.

∎

Lemma 5.5.2
.

For any
k\leq l
, the following equality of subfields of
\mathbb{C}(U(w))
holds:

\mathbb{C}\biggl(x_{\alpha^{\min}}\,,\,D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}\,:\,j\leq k,j\neq\alpha^{\min}\biggr)=\mathbb{C}\biggl(x_{\alpha^{\min}}\,,\,x_{j}\,:\,j\leq k\biggr).

In particular, the functions
\{D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}\,:\,j\leq l,j\neq\alpha^{\min}\}
are algebraically independent.

Proof.

Recall that
x_{j}=D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{e,z_{\leq j}^{-1}}
. We prove the equality by induction on
k
. For
k=0
there is nothing to prove. Suppose that the equality has been proved for
k
and call

\mathbb{F}_{k}=\mathbb{C}\biggl(x_{\alpha^{\min}}\,,\,D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}\,:\,j\leq k,j\neq\alpha^{\min}\biggr)=\mathbb{C}\biggl(x_{\alpha^{\min}}\,,\,x_{j}\,:\,j\leq k\biggr).

If
k+1=\alpha^{\min}
, there is nothing to prove. Similarly, if
i_{k+1}\neq\alpha
, then by Lemma
5.2.1

D^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k+1}^{-1}}=D^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{e,z_{\leq k+1}^{-1}},

so the desired equality is trivial. Next, suppose that
i_{k+1}=\alpha
and
k+1\neq\alpha^{\min}
. Applying Theorem
5.2.2
with
v=e
and
w=z_{\leq k}^{-1}
, we get an equality that can be read as

fD^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k+1}^{-1}}=gD^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{e,z_{\leq k+1}^{-1}}+\varphi.

(46)

We analyse the three terms
\varphi,f,g
.

Note that

\varphi=\prod_{\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}}(D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}.

For
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}
, if
s_{\beta}\not\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z_{\leq k}^{-1})
, then
D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}=1
. Otherwise, let
k_{\beta}=\max\{1\leq j\leq k\,:\,i_{j}=\beta\}
. Then,

D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}=D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,z_{\leq k_{\beta}}^{-1}}=x_{k_{\beta}}.

Hence
\varphi\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
.

Next, we look at
g
. Let
t=(k+1)^{-}
, which is not zero since
k+1\neq\alpha^{\min}
. Lemma
5.2.1
implies that:

g=D^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{{}^{-1}}}=D^{\varpi_{i_{t}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq t}^{{}^{-1}}}.

Since
s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z_{\leq t}^{-1})
, then
g\neq 0
by Lemma
5.4.1
and statement
5
of Lemma
5.2.1
. Moreover, if
t\neq\alpha^{\min}
, we clearly have
g\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
. If
t=\alpha^{\min}
, we deduce that
g\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
from Lemma
5.5.1
.

Finally, we consider
f
. Notice that

f=D^{\varpi_{i_{k+1}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}{{}^{-1}}}=D^{\varpi_{i_{t}}}_{e,z_{\leq t}^{{}^{-1}}}=x_{t}.

In particular, if
t=\alpha^{\min}
, then
f=x_{\alpha^{\min}}\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
. In the other case, it’s obvious that
f\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
. Moreover,
f
is clearly non-zero since it is a cluster variable of
t
.

Since
f,g,\varphi\in\mathbb{F}_{k}
and
f,g
are non-zero, the equality of fields for
k+1
follows by induction from (
46
).

For the algebraic independence, note that the functions
\{x_{j}\,:\,j\leq l\}
are the cluster variables of
t
, which are algebraically independent. The statement follows from the well definiteness of the transcendence degree.
∎

Left twist: the case of
U(z)_{\setminus\alpha}

For a
T
-stable subgroup
H
of
U
and
\alpha\in\Delta
, we set

H_{\setminus\alpha}=H\cap s_{\alpha}Us_{\alpha}.

We fix
\alpha\in\Delta\cap\Phi(z)
. In particular,
s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z)
. In this subsection, for
\beta\in\Delta
and
v,w\in W
, we denote by
D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
the restriction of
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
to
U(z)_{\setminus\alpha}
.

Lemma 5.5.3
.

The following equality holds

\displaystyle D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq\alpha^{\min}}^{-1}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\alpha^{\min}-1}(D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}})^{c_{j}}

for some non-negative integers
c_{j}
. Moreover, for
j<\alpha^{\min}
, we have that
D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}=D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{e,z_{\leq j}^{-1}}
.

Proof.

This is an obvious consequence of Lemma
5.5.1
.
∎

Lemma 5.5.4
.

The functions
\{D^{\varpi_{i_{j}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq j}^{-1}}\,:\,j\leq l,j\neq\alpha^{\min}\}
are algebraically independent.

Proof.

Lemma
5.4.5
implies that
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{s_{\alpha},w}
is invariant by left multiplication of
U(s_{\alpha})
. Since the product induces an isomorphism
U(s_{\alpha})\times U(z)_{\setminus\alpha}\simeq U(z)
by
[
Hum90
]
[Proposition 28.1], we deduce the statement from Lemma
5.5.2
.
∎

Right twist

We fix
\alpha\in\Delta\cap\Phi(z)
. We set

\alpha(-)=\min\{k\,:\,z_{\leq k}\alpha\in\Phi^{-}\}.

(47)

Lemma 5.5.5
.

We have that

1.

\{k\,:\,z_{\leq k}\alpha\in\Phi^{-}\}=\{\alpha(-),\dots,l\}.

2.

s_{\alpha}z_{\leq\alpha(-)}^{-1}=z_{\leq(\alpha(-)-1)}^{-1}
.

3.

\langle-z_{\leq\alpha(-)}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{\alpha(-)}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=1.

4.

The expression
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathbf{i}
by deleting the term
i_{\alpha(-)}
is a reduced expression for
zs_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

1.

By the definition of
\alpha(-)
, we clearly have that
z_{\leq k}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
for
k<\alpha(-)
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{i}
is a reduced expression, from the definition of
z_{\leq k}
it follows that
\Phi(z_{\leq k})\subseteq\Phi(z_{\leq k+1})
. Hence, for
k\geq\alpha(-)
we have that
z_{\leq k}\alpha\in\Phi^{-}.

2.

Since
z_{\leq(\alpha(-)-1)}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
and
s_{i_{\alpha(-)}}z_{\leq(\alpha(-)-1)}\alpha\in\Phi^{-}
, we have that

z_{\leq(\alpha(-)-1)}\alpha=i_{\alpha(-)},

(48)

where we assume that
z_{\leq(\alpha(-)-1)}=e
if
\alpha(-)=1
. Then the identity follows from the fact that, for any
w\in W
and
\beta\in\Phi
, we have that
ws_{\beta}w{{}^{-1}}=s_{w\beta}.

3.

Using (
48
), we can compute that

\begin{array}[]{r l}\langle-z_{\leq\alpha(-)}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{\alpha(-)}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=&\langle-\varpi_{i_{\alpha(-)}},(z_{\leq\alpha(-)}\alpha)^{\vee}\rangle\\
=&\langle-\varpi_{i_{\alpha(-)}},(s_{i_{\alpha(-)}}i_{\alpha(-)})^{\vee}\rangle\\
=&\langle-\varpi_{i_{\alpha(-)}},(-i_{\alpha(-)})^{\vee}\rangle\\
=&1.\end{array}

4.

Using the second statement (and paying attention if
\alpha(-)=1
or
\alpha(-)=l
), we have that

\displaystyle s_{i_{l}}\dots s_{i_{\alpha(-)+1}}s_{i_{\alpha(-)-1}}\dots s_{i_{1}}=s_{i_{l}}\dots s_{i_{\alpha(-)+1}}s_{i_{\alpha(-)}}s_{i_{\alpha(-)-1}}\dots s_{i_{1}}s_{\alpha}=zs_{\alpha}.

Hence,
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
is an expression for
zs_{\alpha}
. Since
\ell(zs_{\alpha})=\ell(z)-1
, we deduce that
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
is reduced.

∎

Here, for
\beta\in\Delta
and
v,w\in W
, we denote by
D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
the restriction of
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}
to
U(z)
. For
k\in[l]
, we define
f_{k}\in\mathbb{C}[U(z)]
as

f_{k}=\begin{cases}D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}&\text{if}\quad(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\\
D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,s_{\alpha}z_{\leq k}^{-1}}&\text{if}\quad(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{-}\end{cases}

Lemma 5.5.6
.

In the above setting,
f_{\alpha(-)}=f_{\alpha(-)^{-}}
. We use the convention that
f_{0}=1.

Proof.

Let
k=\alpha(-)
. By statement 2 of Lemma
5.5.5
we have that

\begin{array}[]{r l}s_{\alpha}z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}}=&z_{\leq k-1}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}}\\
=&s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{(k-1)}}\varpi_{i_{k}}\\
=&s_{i_{i}}\dots s_{i_{k^{-}}}\varpi_{i_{k}}\\
=&z_{\leq k^{-}}\varpi_{i_{k}}\end{array}

where
z_{\leq k^{-}}=e
if
k^{-}=0.
The lemma follows form Lemma
5.2.1
.
∎

Lemma 5.5.7
.

If
-z\alpha\in\Delta
, then
\{f_{k}\,:\,k\neq\alpha(-)\}
are algebraically independent.

Proof.

If
z\alpha=-\beta
, with
\beta\in\Delta
, it means that
zs_{\alpha}=s_{\beta}z\leq_{L}z
, where
\leq_{L}
denotes the
left
weak order on
W
.
In particular,
\Phi(zs_{\alpha})\subseteq\Phi(z)
, which implies that
U(zs_{\alpha})\subseteq U(z).
For
k\in\{1,\dots,l-1\}
let
x^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}[U(zs_{\alpha})]
be the
k
-th cluster variable of the seed
t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
is the reduced expression in Lemma
5.5.5
. Clearly, for
k<\alpha(-)
,
f_{k}
restricts to
x^{\prime}_{k}
, while using the second statement of Lemma
5.5.5
one deduces (analogously to the proof of the fourth statement of Lemma
5.5.5
) that, for
k>\alpha(-)
,
f_{k}
restricts to
x^{\prime}_{k-1}
. Since the cluster variables
x_{k}^{\prime}
are algebraically independent in
\mathbb{C}[U(zs_{\alpha})]
, the lemma follows.
∎

6
Application to G

Let
G
be a semi-simple, simply connected, complex algebraic group. We show here a simple application, of the minimal monomial lifting, to
G
. This example is designed to make the equality between
\mathbb{C}[G]
and the upper cluster algebra constructed via the minimal monomial lifting to fail (see Remark
6.0.5
). It may be useful, for the reader, to have a look at this example to understand the strategy of Section
8.1.1
and
8.2.1
.
We use the notation of Section
5
.

Let
(-)^{T}:G{\,\longrightarrow\,}G
be the
transpose
, which is the involutive anti-automorphism considered in
[
FZ99
, Section 2.1]
. In particular, it is the only anti-automorphism of algebraic groups defined by

x_{\alpha}(t)^{T}=x_{-\alpha}(t)\quad h^{T}=h\quad\text{for any}\,\,\alpha\in\Delta,\,\,t\in\mathbb{C},\,\,h\in T.

This restricts to an anti-isomorphism between
U^{-}
and
U
. If
\mathbf{i}\in R(w_{0})
, let
t_{\mathbf{i}}^{-}
be the seed of
\mathbb{C}[U^{-}]
obtained by "transposing" the seed
t_{\mathbf{i}}
of
\mathbb{C}[U]
. Clearly,
\mathcal{A}(t_{\mathbf{i}}^{-})=\overline{\mathcal{A}}(t_{\mathbf{i}}^{-})=\mathbb{C}[U^{-}]
. Moreover, by
[
FZ99
, Proposition 2.7]
, the cluster variables of
t_{\mathbf{i}}^{-}
are all obtained by restricting functions of the form
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{w,e}
, with
s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(w)
, to
U^{-}
.

Fix
\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}\in R(w_{0})
and call

t_{\mathbf{i}}=(I_{uf},I_{sf},I_{hf},B,x)\quad t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}=(I^{-}_{uf},I^{-}_{sf},I^{-}_{hf},B^{-},x^{-})\quad t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}}=(J_{uf},J_{sf},J_{hf},C,z),

where
t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}}
is the disjoint union of
t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}
and
t_{\mathbf{i}}
, as defined in Section
2.2
. By Lemma
2.2.3
, we have that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbb{C}[U^{-}\times U]
. This is a factorial
\mathbb{C}
-algebra of finite type.

Let
D=\Delta
,
Y=U^{-}\times U
and

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\phi:T\times U^{-}\times U&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\\
(h,u^{-},u)&\longmapsto&u^{-}hu.\end{array}

Moreover, for
\alpha\in D
, we set
X_{\alpha}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}
.

Lemma 6.0.1
.

The triple
(G,\phi,X)
is suitable for
D
-lifting.

Proof.

Since
G
is semi-simple and simply connected,
\mathbb{C}[G]
is a factorial domain, hence normal. Moreover,
G
is of finite type. Condition 1 of Definition
4.0.1
then clearly holds. Condition 2 holds because of Lemma
5.2.3
. Moreover,
\phi
is an open embedding with image
G_{0}=B^{-}B
and, for any
\alpha\in D
,
\phi^{*}(X_{\alpha})=\varpi_{\alpha}\otimes 1
by Lemma
5.2.1
. Hence condition 3 of Definition
4.0.1
holds.
∎

Proposition 6.0.2
.

For any
\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}\in R(w_{0})
we have

\overline{\mathcal{A}}\bigl(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}|t_{\mathbf{i}})\bigr)=\mathbb{C}[G_{0}]\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathcal{A}}\bigl(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}|t_{\mathbf{i}})^{D}\bigr)\subseteq\mathbb{C}[G].

Proof.

Because of Lemma
6.0.1
and Lemma
4.0.9
we can apply Theorem
4.0.3
.
∎

It’s easy to compute the minimal lifting matrix
\nu
and the corresponding cluster
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptz
in this example. Let
\chi:J{\,\longrightarrow\,}\Delta
be the map defined as follows: for
k\in I
(resp.
k\in I^{-}
),
\chi(k)=\alpha
if and only if the minor defining
x_{k}
(See (
38
)) is of the form
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,w}
(resp.
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{w,e})
.

Lemma 6.0.3
.

For any
j\in J
and
\alpha\in D
, we have
\nu_{\alpha,j}=\delta_{\alpha,\chi(j)}
. Moreover, the variable
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptz_{j}
is the generalized minor defining
z_{j}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
j\in I
. If
j\in I^{-}
the proof is similar. Let
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}
be the minor defining
x_{j}
. In particular,
\chi(j)=\beta
. Using Lemma
5.2.1
and the definition of
z_{j}
we have that, for
x=(h,u^{-},u)\in T\times Y

\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}\bigl(\phi(x)\bigr)=\varpi_{\beta}(h)\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}(u)=\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,e}\bigl(\phi(x)\bigr)\bigl(1\otimes z_{j}(\phi(x)\bigr).

In particular, identifying
1\otimes z_{j}
as a rational function on
G
via
\phi
, we have that

\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}=X_{\beta}(1\otimes z_{j}).

(49)

We claim that for any
\alpha\in D
,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w})=0
. Thanks to (
49
), this completes the proof. Suppose by contradiction that there exists
\alpha\in D
such that
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w})>0.

Since
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}
is irreducible in
\mathbb{C}[G]
because of Lemma
5.2.3
, and so is
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}
, this means that
\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,w}=\psi\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}
, for some
\psi\in\mathbb{C}[G]^{*}
. Since
G
is semi-simple,
\psi
is constant. By statement three of Lemma
5.2.1
, we deduce that

(\varpi_{\beta},w\varpi_{\beta})=(\varpi_{\alpha},\varpi_{\alpha}).

So
\alpha=\beta
and
w\varpi_{\beta}=\varpi_{\beta}.
The latter equality gives a contradiction since
s_{\beta}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(w).

∎

From the previous lemma, we deduce that for
j\in I
,
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptz_{j}
is a generalised minor of the form
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,w}
. Similarly, if
j\in I^{-}
,
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptz_{j}
is a generalised minor of the form
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{w,e}
. In both cases,
s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(w)
.

Proposition 6.0.4
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{i}
and
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
start with the same simple root, that is
i_{1}=\alpha=i^{\prime}_{1}
. Then, the upper cluster algebra
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})^{D})
is strictly contained in
\mathbb{C}[G]
.

Proof.

Form the definition of the seeds
t_{\mathbf{i}}
,
t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}
and the previous lemma, we have that
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,s_{\alpha}}
and
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},e}
are cluster variables of the initial seed
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})
. But then, by Theorem
5.2.2
, we have that

\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}}=\frac{\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},e}\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,s_{\alpha}}+\prod_{\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,e})^{-a_{\beta,\alpha}}}{\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}}

(50)

which proves that
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}}
is an element of
\mathbb{C}[G]\setminus\overline{\mathcal{A}}\bigl(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})^{D}\bigr)
. In fact, on the RHS of (
50
) we have an element of
{\mathcal{L}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}}))
which is not in
{\mathcal{L}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})^{D})
. Alternatively, on the LHS of (
50
) we have an irreducible element of
\mathbb{C}[G]
, in particular
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}})\geq 0
(it’s easy to see that it’s actually zero). But by looking at the RHS, we see that
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{s_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}})=-1
. The statement follows from Proposition
4.1.3
.

∎

Remark 6.0.5
.

Note that, for any
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in R(w_{0})
, the seed
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})
is not one of the seeds constructed in
[
BFZ05
]
for the open double Bruhat cell. Moreover, there is no
T
-action on
G
that makes the map
\phi
equivariant. Hence, the equality between
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt(t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{-}|t_{\mathbf{i}})^{D})
and
\mathbb{C}[G]
is never reached, for any pair
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in R(w_{0})
, because of Corollary
4.1.6
. Finally, a similar construction can be done for the spherical homogeneous space
G/{\widehat{G}}
if and only if the pair
{\widehat{G}}\subseteq G
is of minimal rank. This will be developed in the future.

7
Monomial lifting for branching problems

In this section we explain how to apply the minimal monomial lifting technique to study some branching problems.

7.1
The branching scheme

Let
G
be a semisimple, simply connected complex algebraic group and let
{\widehat{G}}
be a connected, reductive subgroup of
G
. Fix maximal tori and Borel subgroups
T
,
B
of
G
and
{\widehat{T}},{\widehat{B}}
of
{\widehat{G}}
such that
T\cap{\widehat{G}}={\widehat{T}}
and
B\cap{\widehat{G}}={\widehat{B}}
. Let
\rho:X(T){\,\longrightarrow\,}X({\widehat{T}})
be the restriction map.
Consider the
U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}
action on
G\times{\widehat{G}}
defined by

(u^{-},\widehat{u},\widehat{s})\cdot(g,\widehat{g})=(u^{-}g\widehat{s}{{}^{-1}},\widehat{s}\widehat{g}\widehat{u}{{}^{-1}}).

By (
26
), we clearly have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}}\simeq\bigoplus(V(\lambda)\otimes V(\widehat{\lambda})^{*})^{\widehat{G}}\simeq\bigoplus{\operatorname{Hom}}(V(\widehat{\lambda}),V(\lambda))^{\widehat{G}},

(51)

where the sums run over
(\lambda,\widehat{\lambda})\in X(T)^{+}\times X({\widehat{T}})^{+}.
Recall that, if
\lambda\in X(T)\setminus X(T)^{+}
(resp.
\widehat{\lambda}\in X({\widehat{T}})\setminus X({\widehat{T}})^{+}
), then we set
V(\lambda)=0
(resp.
V(\widehat{\lambda})=0
). Consider the
T\times{\widehat{T}}
action on
G\times{\widehat{G}}
defined by

(h,\widehat{h})\cdot(g,\widehat{g})=(hg,\widehat{g}\widehat{h}).

The induced action on
\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]
stabilises
\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}}
which is then
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graded. It’s easy to verify that the homogeneous components of this graduation correspond to the decomposition given in (
51
).
In particular, for any
(\lambda,\widehat{\lambda})\in X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
, we have

\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}}_{\lambda,\widehat{\lambda}}=(V(\lambda)\otimes V(\widehat{\lambda})^{*})^{\widehat{G}}.

If
\lambda
and
\widehat{\lambda}
are dominant, then
(V(\lambda)\otimes V(\widehat{\lambda})^{*})^{\widehat{G}}
is the
multiplicity space
of the representation
V(\widehat{\lambda})
in
V(\lambda)
, the latter considered as a representation of
{\widehat{G}}
.

Consider now the action of
U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}
on
G
given by
(u^{-},\widehat{u})\cdot g=(u^{-}g\widehat{u}{{}^{-1}})
. The action of
T\times{\widehat{T}}
on
G
, given by
(h,\widehat{h})\cdot g=hg\widehat{h}
, stabilised
\mathbb{C}[G]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}}
and thus induces a
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graduation on it.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

.
The product
G\times{\widehat{G}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}G
induces a
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant isomorphism between
\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}}
and
\mathbb{C}[G]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}}
.

Proof.

The inverse is the map
G{\,\longrightarrow\,}G\times{\widehat{G}}
sending
g
to
(g,e)
. The proof is straightforward.
∎

From now on, we identify the two graded algebras appearing in the previous lemma. We define the
branching algebra

{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
to be the
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graded algebra

{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}}):=\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{G}}}=\mathbb{C}[G]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}}.

(52)

Lemma 7.1.2
.

The branching algebra
{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
is factorial and of finite type. An element
f\in{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
is irreducible if and only if it is irreducible as an element of
\mathbb{C}[G].

Proof.

The group
U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}
is connected, has no non-trivial character. Moreover,
\mathbb{C}[G]
is factorial since
G
is semisimple and simply connected. Then, the factoriality of
{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
and the last statement follow from Lemma
5.2.4
.
Moreover, it is well known that
\mathbb{C}[G\times{\widehat{G}}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}}=\mathbb{C}[G]^{U^{-}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[{\widehat{G}}]^{\widehat{U}}
is of finite type. Since
{\widehat{G}}
is reductive, then
{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
is of finite type by a well known theorem of Hilbert.
∎

Then, we define the
branching scheme

{\mathfrak{X}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
as the normal, integral and affine
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-variety

{\mathfrak{X}}(G,{\widehat{G}}):={\operatorname{Spec}}({\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})).

(53)

When
G
and
{\widehat{G}}
are fixed, we drop the dependence on
G,{\widehat{G}}
and just write
{\mathfrak{X}}
for
{\mathfrak{X}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
and
{\operatorname{Br}}
for
{\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}}).

7.2
A first suitable for lifting structure

In the setting of the previous section, we want to prove that
{\mathfrak{X}}={\mathfrak{X}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
is homogeneously suitable for lifting. We fix

D=\Delta

(54)

and for any
\alpha\in D
, we set

X_{\alpha}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}\in\mathbb{C}[G]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}}={\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}}).

(55)

Let

\Omega={\mathfrak{X}}_{\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta}X_{\alpha}}={\mathfrak{X}}\setminus\bigcup_{\alpha\in D}V(X_{\alpha})

be the principal open subscheme of
{\mathfrak{X}}
defined by the non-vanishing the
X_{\alpha}
.

Moreover, let
\widetilde{Y}:={\operatorname{Spec}}(\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}})
, where
{\widehat{U}}
acts on
U
by right multiplication. The
{\widehat{T}}
action on
U
defined by
\widehat{h}\cdot u=\widehat{h}{{}^{-1}}u\widehat{h}
defines a structure of
{\widehat{T}}
-scheme on
\widetilde{Y}
. Similarly, the action of
T\times{\widehat{T}}
on
T\times U
defined by

(h,\widehat{h})\cdot(t,u)=(ht\widehat{h}\,,\,\widehat{h}{{}^{-1}}u\widehat{h})

(56)

factors through a
T\times{\widehat{T}}
action on
T\times\widetilde{Y}
.
By definition, the induced
T
action on
T\times\widetilde{Y}
is the left multiplication of
T
on the
T
-component.

Lemma 7.2.1
.

The product map
T\times U{\,\longrightarrow\,}G
induces an open embedding
\widetilde{\phi}:T\times\widetilde{Y}{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}
, with image
\Omega
. Moreover, the following hold.

1.

For any
\alpha\in D
,
\widetilde{\phi}^{*}(X_{\alpha})=\varpi_{\alpha}\otimes 1
.

2.

The map
\widetilde{\phi}
is
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant.

Proof.

By
[
FZ99
, Cororllary 2.5]
, the non vanishing locus in
G
of all the generalised minors
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}
is
G_{0}
. In particular,
\Omega={\operatorname{Spec}}(\mathbb{C}[G_{0}]^{U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}})
. Recall that the product induces an isomorphism
U^{-}\times T\times U\simeq G_{0}
. Then, the proof consists of some immediate verifications. Note that the inverse of
\widetilde{\phi}:T\times\widetilde{Y}{\,\longrightarrow\,}\Omega
is induced by the map
G_{0}{\,\longrightarrow\,}T\times U
sending
x
to
([x]_{0},[x]_{+}).

∎

Proposition 7.2.2
.

The triple
({\mathfrak{X}},\widetilde{\phi},X)
is homogeneously-suitable for
D
-lifting.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Lemmas
7.1.2
,
5.2.3
and
7.2.1
.
∎

The following question naturally arises.

Question 7.2.3
.

For which pairs
(G,{\widehat{G}})
the algebra
\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}
has a cluster structure?

7.3
Lifting graduation for the Branching algebra

Before describing some pairs
(G,{\widehat{G}})
for which we can answer Question
7.2.3
, we take a closer look at the information given by the lifting graduation on
{\operatorname{Br}}
.

Note that, by Lemma
3.1.6
, the
X(T)
-graduation on
{\operatorname{Br}}
is a lifting graduation of the pole filtration on
\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}
induced by the almost polynomial ring
({\mathfrak{X}},\mathcal{V},X,\widetilde{\phi}^{*})
.
Note that the conjugation of
T
on
U
:

t\cdot u=t{{}^{-1}}ut\quad\text{for}\quad t\in T,\,u\in U

factors through an action of
{\widehat{T}}
on
\widetilde{Y}
.

Lemma 7.3.1
.

The pole filtration on
\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}
is
X({\widehat{T}})
-graded.

Proof.

Identify
\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
and
\mathbb{C}(T\times\widetilde{Y})
using
\widetilde{\phi}
. Recall that
\mathbb{Z}^{D}
is canonically identified with
X(T)
via the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map defined by
e_{\alpha}\longmapsto\varpi_{\alpha}.
Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, then

f\in\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\lambda}{\,\Longleftrightarrow\,}X^{\lambda}(1\otimes f)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{X}}({\mathfrak{X}}),

(57)

where
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\lambda}
is the
\lambda
-component of the lifting graduation. The map
\mathbb{C}(\widetilde{Y})\longmapsto\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
defined by
f\longmapsto 1\otimes f
is
{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant. Since, for any
\alpha\in D
,
X_{\alpha}
is
{\widehat{T}}
semi-invariant, we deduce from (
57
) that
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\lambda}
is
{\widehat{T}}
-stable.
∎

The
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graduation on
{\operatorname{Br}}
is NOT a
X({\widehat{T}})
-graded lifting graduation. Indeed, the map
\widetilde{Y}{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}
defined by
\widetilde{y}\longmapsto\widetilde{\phi}((e,\widetilde{y}))
is not
{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant (see Lemma
3.1.5
). We can fix the situation by twisting the
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-action on
{\mathfrak{X}}.

Let
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}
be the scheme
{\mathfrak{X}}
endowed with the twisted
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-action
*
defined by

(h,\widehat{h})*x=(h\widehat{h}{{}^{-1}},\widehat{h})\cdot x,

where
\cdot
denotes the standard
T\times{\widehat{T}}
-action on
{\mathfrak{X}}.
We denote by
{\operatorname{Br}}^{*}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}}({\mathfrak{X}}^{*})
, considered with its
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graduation induced by the
*
-action. As
\mathbb{C}
-algebras,
{\operatorname{Br}}^{*}={\operatorname{Br}}.

Note that a subset of
{\mathfrak{X}}
is stable for the
\cdot
action if and only if it is stable for the
*
action.

Corollary 7.3.2
.

The triple
({\mathfrak{X}}^{*},\widetilde{\phi},X)
is homogeneously suitable for
D
-lifting. Moreover, the
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-graduation on
{\operatorname{Br}}^{*}
is a
X({\widehat{T}})
-graded lifting graduation.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is obvious from Proposition
7.2.2
. Moreover, using (
56
), we easily deduce that, for any
h,t\in T
,
\widehat{h}\in{\widehat{T}}
and
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{Y}
, then

(h,\widehat{h})*\widetilde{\phi}\bigl((t,\widetilde{y})\bigr)=\widetilde{\phi}\bigl((ht,\widehat{h}\cdot\widetilde{y})\bigr)

where
h\cdot\widetilde{y}
denotes the conjugation action. In particular, the map
\iota:\widetilde{Y}{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}
defined by
\widetilde{y}\longmapsto\widetilde{\phi}((e,\widetilde{y}))
is
{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant. We conclude by applying Lemma
3.1.5
.
∎

Let
\iota:\widetilde{Y}{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}
be as in the proof of the previous corollary.

Proposition 7.3.3
.

For any
(\lambda,\widehat{\lambda})\in X(T)^{+}\times X({\widehat{T}})^{+}
,
\iota^{*}
induces an isomorphism

{\operatorname{Hom}}(V(\lambda),V(\widehat{\lambda}))^{\widehat{G}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}_{\lambda,\widehat{\lambda}-\rho(\lambda)}

where

\begin{array}[]{r l l }\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}_{\lambda,\widehat{\lambda}-\rho(\lambda)}=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}}\,:&f(\widehat{h}^{{}^{-1}}u\widehat{h})=(\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda)(\widehat{h})f(u)&\text{for}\quad\widehat{h}\in{\widehat{T}},u\in U\ ,\,\text{and}\\
&\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(1\otimes f)\geq-\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle&\text{for}\quad\alpha\in D\}.\end{array}

Proof.

It’s a simple computation that
{\operatorname{Br}}_{\lambda,\widehat{\lambda}}={\operatorname{Br}}^{*}_{\lambda,\widehat{\lambda}-\rho(\lambda)}
. Then the proposition follows from Corollary
7.3.2
and Definition
3.1.4
.
∎

Remark 7.3.4
.

The above proposition gives a geometric realisation of the multiplicity spaces for the branching problem of
{\widehat{G}}
in
G
. Consider the tensor product case, that is when
G={\widehat{G}}\times{\widehat{G}}
and
{\widehat{G}}
is diagonally embedded in
G
. By identifying
U/{\widehat{U}}=({\widehat{U}}\times{\widehat{U}})/{\widehat{U}}
with
{\widehat{U}}
via the map
(\widehat{u}_{1},\widehat{u}_{2})\longmapsto\widehat{u}_{1}\widehat{u}_{2}{{}^{-1}}
, we can easily deduce Proposition 1.4 of
[
Zel02
]
. Actually,
[
Zel99
]
[Proposition 1.4] is the starting point for the interest in perfect bases, which have classically been one of the most used items to study tensor product decomposition. See
[
Kam22
]
for a beautiful survey on perfect bases. We wonder if Proposition
7.3.3
can lead to the development of a similar theory for other branching problems.

7.4
On question
7.2.3

Question
7.2.3
being quite vague, the author feels the following question more interesting.

Question 7.4.1
.

For which pairs
(G,{\widehat{G}})
, there exist a
z\in W
such that the product
U(z)\times{\widehat{U}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}U
is an isomorphism?

If the answer to the previous question is positive for a certain
z\in W
, then the natural map
U(z){\,\longrightarrow\,}\widetilde{Y}
is an isomorphism. Thus,
\widetilde{Y}
has a natural cluster structure obtained throughout this identification. In the following, we exhibit two families for which we can answer positively to Question
7.4.1
. In general, the answer to the previous question is positive in many other interesting cases, as we will see in some forthcoming works.

7.4.1
Levi subgroups

Let
I\subsetneq\Delta
be a strict subset of
\Delta
, and
\Phi_{I}=\Phi\cap\mathbb{Z}I
. This is a root system with
I
as simple set of roots and Weyl group
W_{I}=\langle s_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in I\rangle\subseteq W
. Consider
{\widehat{G}}=G_{I}
the
Levi subgroup
of
G
, which is a reductive group with root system
{\widehat{\Phi}}=\Phi_{I}
. Let
{\widehat{T}}=T={\widehat{G}}\cap T
and let
{\widehat{B}}=B\cap{\widehat{G}}
. Note that
{\widehat{W}}=W_{I}
. Let
w_{0,I}
be the longest element of
{\widehat{W}}
and
z=w_{0,I}^{\vee}=w_{0}w_{0,I}
. We have that
{\widehat{U}}=U(w_{0,I})
. Then we define

Y=U(z)=U(w_{0,I}^{\vee}).

As a special case, when
I=\emptyset
, then
{\widehat{G}}=T
and
Y=U.

7.4.2
Products

Let
{\widehat{G}}
and
H
be semisimple, simply connected, complex algebraic groups such that
{\widehat{G}}\subseteq H
. Let
T_{H}
,
B_{H}
(resp.
{\widehat{T}}
,
{\widehat{B}}
) be a maximal torus and a Borel subgroup of
H
(resp.
{\widehat{G}}
) such that
T_{H}\subseteq B_{H}
(resp.
{\widehat{T}}\subseteq{\widehat{B}})
. Denote by
U_{H}
(resp
{\widehat{U}}
) the unipotent radical of
B_{H}
resp
{\widehat{B}}
. Assume furthermore that
{\widehat{T}}\subseteq T_{H}
and
{\widehat{B}}\subseteq B_{H}
.

Let
G=H\times{\widehat{G}}
, and consider
{\widehat{G}}
diagonally embedded in
G
.
Then,
T=T_{H}\times{\widehat{T}}
,
B=B_{H}\times{\widehat{B}}
and
U=U_{H}\times{\widehat{U}}
(recall our convention on products
5.4.8
).

Let
z=(w_{0,H},e)\in W=W_{H}\times{\widehat{W}}
, where
w_{0,H}
is the longest element of
W_{H}
. Then we set

Y=U(z)=U_{H}\times\{e\}.

Note that, the case of
{\widehat{G}}
diagonally embedded into
{\widehat{G}}^{n}
, which is the product of
n
copies of
{\widehat{G}}
, is a special case of this construction. The corresponding branching problem consists in decomposing the tensor product of
n
irreducible representations of
{\widehat{G}}
, under the diagonal action. Also, the branching problem of
{\widehat{G}}\subseteq H
is completely determined by the one of
{\widehat{G}}\subseteq G
.

Remark 7.4.2
.

Note that the reduced expressions of
z\in W
are in obvious bijection with the reduced expressions of
w_{0,H}\in W_{H}
. The reduced expression
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
gives a seed
t_{\mathbf{i}}
of
\mathbb{C}[U(z)]
. Thinking about the same reduced expression as an expression for
w_{0,H}
, we obtain a seed
t_{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{C}[U_{H}]
. It’s trivial to verify, using the last identity in Remark
5.4.8
, that
t_{\mathbf{i}}=t_{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}
under the obvious identifiction between
U(z)
and
U_{H}.
So, we make no difference between these two seeds.

From now on, we suppose that the pair
{\widehat{G}}\subseteq G
belongs to one of the two cases discussed above. The element
z\in W
, for a given pair, is the one previously described.

Lemma 7.4.3
.

The product
U(z)\times{\widehat{U}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}U
is a
{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant isomorphism. Thus, the natural inclusion
Y=U(z){\,\longrightarrow\,}U
induces a
{\widehat{T}}
-equivariant isomorphism between
Y
and
\widetilde{Y}={\operatorname{Spec}}(\mathbb{C}[U]^{\widehat{U}})
.

Proof.

For the Levi case, the claim follows from
[
Hum75
]
[Proposition 28.1]. For the product case, the above map is given explicitly by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}U_{H}\times{\widehat{U}}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&U_{H}\times{\widehat{U}}\\
(u_{h},\widehat{u})&\longmapsto&(u_{h}\widehat{u},\widehat{u})\end{array}

which is obviously an isomorphism.
∎

Let
\phi:T\times Y{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}
be the map obtained from
\widetilde{\phi}
, by identifying
Y
and
\widetilde{Y}
as in Lemma
7.4.3
.

Corollary 7.4.4
.

The triple
({\mathfrak{X}},\phi,X)
is homogeneously suitable for
D
-lifting.

Proof.

It is obvious from Corollary
7.3.2
and Lemma
7.4.3
.
∎

Recall that
{\operatorname{Br}}^{*}
is the algebra
{\operatorname{Br}}
with its twisted
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
graduation, as defined in Section
7.3
.

Theorem 7.4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
and
\sigma_{\mathbf{i}}
be the
X(T)
-degree configuration on
t_{\mathbf{i}}
defined in Proposition
5.3.4
. We have that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}})={\operatorname{Br}}_{\prod_{\alpha\in D}X_{\alpha}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D})\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}.

Moreover,
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D})
with the graduation induced by
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006pt\rho(\sigma_{\mathbf{i}})
, is a graded subalgebra of
{\operatorname{Br}}^{*}
.

Remark 7.4.6
.

In the notation of Theorem
7.4.5
,
\rho(\sigma_{\mathbf{i}})\in X({\widehat{T}})^{I_{\mathbf{i}}}
is the
X({\widehat{T}})
-degree configuration obtained by applying component-wise the restriction map
\rho:X(T){\,\longrightarrow\,}X({\widehat{T}})
to
\sigma_{\mathbf{i}}.

Proof.

Because of Corollary
7.4.4
and Lemma
4.0.9
we can apply Theorem
4.0.3
. The part on the graduation is a consequence of Lemma
4.1.7
.
∎

From now on, fix
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
as above. Let
t=t_{\mathbf{i}}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{D\times I}
be the minimal lifting matrix of the seed
t
associated to
{\mathfrak{X}}
. Recall that, for any
k\in I
,
\nu_{\bullet,k}
is canonically an element of
X(T).

Lemma 7.4.7
.

For any
k\in I
,
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}
is a
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-homogeneous element of
{\operatorname{Br}}
of degree

\bigl(\nu_{\bullet,k}\,,\,\rho(\nu_{\bullet,k})+\rho(z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}}-\varpi_{i_{k}})\bigr).

Proof.

Because of (
56
) and Lemma
5.2.1
, we easily deduce that
1\otimes x_{k}
is a
X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
-homogeneous rational function of weight
(0\,,\,\rho(z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}}-\varpi_{i_{k}}))
. Moreover, for any
\alpha\in D
,
X_{\alpha}
is homogeneous of degree
(\varpi_{\alpha}\,,\,\rho(\varpi_{\alpha})).
The lemma follows from the definition of
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}.

∎

The previous lemma gives some explicit components of the branching from
G
to
{\widehat{G}}
. Moreover, using the mutation formula for degree configurations (
11
), we obtain a (a priory infinite) set of non-zero components for the branching from
G
to
{\widehat{G}}
. These non-zero components can be computed by explicit recursive formulas.
Also, if
t^{*}
is a graded seed mutation equivalent to
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt^{D}
, for any
k\in I_{uf}
we have that

\sigma^{*}_{k}+\mu_{k}(\sigma_{k}^{*})\in X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})

identifies a component of the branching from
G
to
{\widehat{G}}
of multiplicity at least
2
. Indeed,
\sigma_{k}^{*}+\mu_{k}(\sigma_{k}^{*})
is the degree of
x_{k}^{*}\mu_{k}(x_{k}^{*})
which, by the exchange relation (
3
), can be expressed as a sum of two linearly independent homogeneous elements of degree
\sigma_{k}^{*}+\mu_{k}(\sigma_{k}^{*}).
Similarly, we easily deduce that for any
n\geq 1
,
n\bigl(\sigma^{*}_{k}+\mu_{k}(\sigma_{k}^{*})\bigr)
identifies a component of multiplicity at least
n+1.

The author is curious about the following questions.

Question 7.4.8
.

1.

Let
k\in I_{uf}
. Define
\mathfrak{D}(k)=\{\deg(x_{k}^{*})\in X(T)\times X({\widehat{T}})
:
t^{*}\sim\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt^{D}\}
.
Is the set
\mathfrak{D}(k)
infinite?.

2.

For which
t^{*}\sim\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt^{D}
and
k\in I_{uf}
, if
\deg(x_{k}^{*})=(\lambda,\widehat{\lambda})
, then
\dim{\operatorname{Hom}}(V(\lambda),V(\widehat{\lambda}))^{\widehat{G}}=1.

8
Study of equality

In this section we study whether there is equality, between
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D})
and
{\operatorname{Br}}
, in Theorem
7.4.5
. By Proposition
4.1.3
, for any
\alpha\in D
, we need to compare the valuations
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and the cluster valuations
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
.

Recall that
\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
is naturally identified with a subfield of
\mathbb{C}(G)
. If a rational function
f\in\mathbb{C}(G)
belongs to
\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
, then
f
is invariant for left multiplication by
U^{-}
and for right multiplication by
{\widehat{U}}
.

Lemma 8.0.1
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
and
\alpha\in D
,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}}(f).

Proof.

It’s sufficient to prove the case of
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{X}}({\mathfrak{X}})
. Then,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(f)
is the multiplicity of
X_{\alpha}
, in the decomposition of
f
into irreducible factors in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{X}}({\mathfrak{X}}).
By Lemma
5.2.4
, this is the same as the multiplicity of
X_{\alpha}
in the decomposition of
f
into irreducible factors in
\mathbb{C}[G]
. The statement follows by Lemma
5.2.1
, since
V(X_{\alpha})=\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
.
∎

8.1
The Levi case

The notation of this section is as in Subsection
7.4.1
. We want to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1.1
.

Suppose that
G
is simple and
{\widehat{G}}=G_{I}
is the Levi subgroup corresponding to
I\subsetneq\Delta
. For any
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
, there’s equality in Theorem
7.4.5
. That is
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D})={\operatorname{Br}}(G,G_{I})
.

Note that, the branching problem from a semisimple group to a Levi subgroup, can always be reduced to the study of a finite number of branchings from a simple group, to a Levi subgroup.

Corollary 8.1.2
.

If
G
is simply laced and
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in R(z)
, then
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D}\simeq\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{D}
.

Proof.

Since
G
is simply laced,
t_{\mathbf{i}}\simeq t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
. Then
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D}\simeq\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{D}
by Theorem
8.1.1
, Corollary
4.0.11
and Lemma
3.0.8
.
∎

From now on,
{\widehat{G}}
and
G
are as in the hypothesis of Theorem
8.1.1
and
\mathbf{i}\in R(z)
is fixed. Recall that
z=w_{0,I}^{\vee}=w_{0}w_{0,I}
. We denote
t=t_{\mathbf{i}}
and
{\operatorname{Br}}={\operatorname{Br}}(G,{\widehat{G}})
. Here
D=\Delta
and we have a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{-}\times T\times Y\times{\widehat{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(58)

where the leftmost vertical map is defined by
(u^{-},h,y,\widehat{u})\longmapsto(h,y)
,
\psi
by
(u^{-},h,y,\widehat{u})\longmapsto u^{-}hy\widehat{u}
and
\pi
is the natural projection. Note that the image of
\psi
is
G_{0}.

Recall that, for a
T
-stable subgroup
H
of
U
and
\alpha\in\Delta
,
H_{\setminus\alpha}=H\cap s_{\alpha}Us_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 8.1.3
.

For
\alpha\in D
, let
\iota_{\alpha}
be the map defined by

1.

If
\alpha\in I

\begin{array}[]{c c l}\iota_{\alpha}:U^{-}\times T\times\mathbb{C}\times Y\times{\widehat{U}}_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\\
x=(u^{-},h,t,y,\widehat{u})&\longmapsto&\iota_{\alpha}(x)=u^{-}h\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)y\widehat{u}.\end{array}

2.

If
\alpha\not\in I

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\iota_{\alpha}:U^{-}\times T\times\mathbb{C}\times Y_{\setminus\alpha}\times{\widehat{U}}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\\
x=(u^{-},h,t,y,\widehat{u})&\longmapsto&\iota_{\alpha}(x)=u^{-}h\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)y\widehat{u}.\end{array}

Then,
\iota_{\alpha}
is an open embedding whose image intersects
\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
. Moreover,
\iota_{\alpha}(x)\in\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
if and only if
t=0
, otherwivse
\iota_{\alpha}(x)\in G_{0}
.

Proof.

It’s clear that, for any
\alpha\in\Delta
, the map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha}:U^{-}\times T\times U&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\\
(u^{-},h,u)&\longmapsto&u^{-}hu\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}\end{array}

(59)

is an open embedding whose image intersects
\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
. Moreover, the map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\mathbb{C}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&U\\
(t,u)&\longmapsto&x_{\alpha}(-t)u\end{array}

is an isomorphism and conjugation by
\overline{s}_{\alpha}
induces an automorphism of
U_{\setminus\alpha}
.
To conclude that
\iota_{\alpha}
is an open embedding, note first that the product induces an isomorphism between
Y\times{\widehat{U}}_{\setminus\alpha}
(resp.
Y_{\setminus\alpha}\times{\widehat{U}}
) and
U_{\setminus\alpha}
, if
\alpha\in I
(resp
\alpha\not\in I)
, because of
[
Hum90
, Proposition 28.1]
. Finally, we use that for any
t\in\mathbb{C}
,

x_{\alpha}(-t)\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}=\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t),

which is an easy identity that can be checked in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. It’s clear from the definition of
\iota_{\alpha}
that
\iota_{\alpha}(x)\in\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
if the
t
-coordinate of
x
is zero. If
t\neq 0
,
\iota_{\alpha}(x)\in G_{0}
because of the second identity in (
31
).
∎

By the previous lemma, the divisor
\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
, in the chart given by
\iota_{\alpha}
, corresponds to
\{t=0\}.
Then we make a very important computation.

Lemma 8.1.4
.

Let
\lambda\in X(T)
,
\alpha\in D
and
f\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{\lambda,\bullet}.
For a generic
x
as in the notation of Lemma
8.1.3
, such that
t\neq 0
, we have

f(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\lambda(h)t^{\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}f(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y).

Moreover, if
\beta\in D
, then

X_{\beta}(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h)t^{\langle\varpi_{\beta},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}.

Remark 8.1.5
.

With little abuse, for
\lambda\in X(T)
, we denote by
\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{\lambda,\bullet}
the set of
T
-equivariant rational functions of weight
\lambda.

Proof.

If
x=(u^{-},h,t,y,\widehat{u})
, using the second formula in (
31
) and the fact that
f
is
U^{-}\times{\widehat{U}}
-invariant, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rl}f(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=&f(h\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)y)\\
=&\lambda(h)f(x_{-\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}})\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)\\
=&\lambda(h)t^{\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}f(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y).\end{array}

The statement on
X_{\beta}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{e,e}
follows immediately from Lemma
5.2.1
.

∎

We use the convention that, whenever
\alpha\in D
is fixed, for any
v,w\in W
and
\beta\in\Delta
, we denote

D^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w}=\begin{cases}(\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w})_{|Y}&\text{if}\,\,\alpha\in I\\
(\Delta^{\varpi_{\beta}}_{v,w})_{|Y_{\setminus\alpha}}&\text{if}\,\,\alpha\not\in I.\end{cases}

(60)

Proposition 8.1.6
.

Let
k\in I
and
\alpha\in D
. Then
\nu_{\alpha,k}=\delta_{\alpha,i_{k}}.
Moreover for
x
as in Lemma
8.1.3

\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}\bigl(\iota_{\alpha}(x)\bigr)=\varpi_{i_{k}}(h)\biggl(p_{k,0}(y)+tp_{k,1}(y)\biggr)\quad\text{where}\quad p_{k,0}=D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}.

Moreover:

p_{k,1}=\begin{cases}0&\textit{if}\quad\alpha\neq i_{k}\\
D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 8.1.7
.

Note that
p_{k,0}
and
p_{k,1}
depend on
\alpha.
So, when we use such a notation, we assume that it refers to an
\alpha
which is sufficiently clear from the context.

Proof.

First, we compute
\nu.

Suppose that
\alpha\in I
and that
x
is such that
t\neq 0
. Since
1\otimes x_{k}\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{0,\bullet}
, from Lemma
8.1.4
we deduce that
x_{k}(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)
. Recall that, in this case,
y\in Y=U(z).
Note that conjugation by
x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})
stabilises
Y
.
In fact, if
\delta\in\Phi(z)=\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{I}
and
\delta+\alpha\in\Phi
, then
\delta+\alpha\in\Phi(z).
Hence

\begin{array}[]{rl }1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)&=1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}})x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}}))\\
&=1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))\end{array}

where the last equality follows from the fact that, if
\alpha\in I
, then
x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})\in{\widehat{U}}
. Note that (see (
58
))

\phi\biggl((e,x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))\biggr)=\pi\bigl(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}})\bigr).

In particular, from the definition of
1\otimes x_{k}
we deduce the first of the following equalities

\begin{array}[]{rl}1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))=&x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))\\[5.0pt]
=&\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})yx_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))\\[8.99994pt]
=&\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y).\end{array}

The second equality is the definition of
x_{k}
and the last one follows from Lemma
5.4.3
and the fact that
z_{\leq k}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. In fact, from the definition of
z_{\leq k}
, we have that
z_{\leq k}\leq_{L}z
, where
\leq_{L}
denotes the left weak order. Hence,
\Phi(z_{\leq k})\subseteq\Phi(z)
and
\alpha\not\in\Phi(z).
Now we apply Lemma
5.4.7
.

If
i_{k}\neq\alpha
, we deduce that

\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)=\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y)=\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y).

The last equality follows from Lemma
5.2.1
, since
s_{\alpha}\varpi_{i_{k}}=\varpi_{i_{k}}
. In particular, we deduce that
\nu_{\alpha,k}=0.

If
i_{k}=\alpha
, we have that

\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)=\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y)+t{{}^{-1}}\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y).

By Lemma
5.4.1
, we have that
\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}
doesn’t vanish on
U
. In fact,
s_{i_{k}}=s_{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z_{\leq k}^{-1})
. Then, by Lemma
5.2.1
,
\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}
doesn’t vanish on

U\cap s_{\alpha}Us_{\alpha}\cap z_{\leq k}^{-1}U^{-}z_{\leq k}\subseteq U\cap z{{}^{-1}}U^{-}z=Y.

We deduce that
\nu_{\alpha,k}=1.

We assume now that
\alpha\not\in I
. In this case,
x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})\in Y
, so for
y\in Y_{\setminus\alpha}
we have that
x_{\alpha}(t^{{}^{-1}})y\in Y
. In particular, by a very similar but simpler argument then the previous one, we have that

1\otimes x(k)(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=1\otimes x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)=\Delta^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y).

Then, literally the same proof of the previous case, up to replacing
Y
with
Y_{\setminus\alpha}
, implies that
\nu_{\alpha,k}=\delta_{\alpha,i_{k}}
.

From the explicit expression of
\nu
, it follows that
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}=X_{i_{k}}(1\otimes x_{k}).
Then, the last part of the statement follows from the previous calculations and Lemma
8.1.4
.
∎

Corollary 8.1.8
.

For any
k\in[l]
,
\rho(z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}})
is a dominant weight and the
{\widehat{G}}
-representation
V\bigl(\rho(z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}})\bigr)
is a sub-representation of
V(\varpi_{i_{k}}).

Proof.

The cluster variable
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
(\varpi_{i_{k}}\,,\,\rho(z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}}))
, because of Lemma
7.4.7
and Proposition
8.1.6
.
∎

Lemma 8.1.9
.

For any
I\subsetneq\Delta
,
{\operatorname{supp}}(z)=\Delta
.

Remark 8.1.10
.

This is the only lemma in which we use that
G
is simple.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
and
\Phi^{+}_{\alpha}=\{\sum_{\beta\in\Delta}n_{\beta}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\,:\,n_{\alpha}>0\}
. If by contradiction
s_{\alpha}\not\in{\operatorname{supp}}(z)
, then
z\Phi^{+}_{\alpha}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
. In fact, for any
\delta\in\Phi
, the
\alpha
-coefficients of
\delta
and
z\delta
(in the base
\Delta
) are the same.

Since
G
is simple, we can consider
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
the longest root of
\Phi
. We have that
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{\beta}
for any
\beta\in\Delta
, hence
\gamma\not\in\Phi(z)
. But then
\gamma\in\Phi(w_{0,I})
. This is a contradiction since
\gamma\not\in\Phi_{I}.

∎

By the previous lemma, for any
\alpha\in\Delta
,
\alpha^{\min}
is well defined.

Lemma 8.1.11
.

In the notation of Proposition
8.1.6

\displaystyle p_{\alpha^{\min},0}=\prod_{j=1}^{\alpha^{\min}-1}p_{j,0}^{c_{j}}

for some non-negative integers
c_{j}
.

Again, the exponents
c_{j}
depend on
\alpha
(as the functions
p_{j,0})
, so we use such a notation when it refers to an
\alpha
which is sufficiently clear from the context.

Proof.

Accordingly to if
\alpha\in I
or not, this is a reformulation of Lemma
5.5.1
or of Lemma
5.5.3
respectively.
∎

Lemma 8.1.12
.

The functions
p_{k,0}
, for
k\neq\alpha^{\min}
and
k\in[l]
, are algebraically independent.

Proof.

Again this is a reformulation of Lemma
5.5.2
or Lemma
5.5.4
.
∎

We are ready to prove Theorem
8.1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
8.1.1
.

By contradiction suppose that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt^{D})
is strictly contained in
{\operatorname{Br}}
. By Proposition
4.1.3
, there exists an
\alpha\in D
and
f\in{\operatorname{Br}}
such that
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(f)<0
. Since
{\operatorname{Br}}
is
X(T)
-graded, we can suppose
f
homogeneous.
Recall that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt^{D})
is a graded subalgebra of
{\operatorname{Br}}
, which in turn is a
X(T)
-graded subalgebra of
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt)
. In particular, up to multiplying for a monomial in the
X_{\beta}
,
\beta\in D
, and in the unfrozen variables of
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx
, we can suppose that there exist
\lambda\in X(T)
and
f\in{\operatorname{Br}}_{\lambda,\bullet}
such that

f=\frac{P}{\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{\alpha}}\quad\text{where}\quad P=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.57646pt\kern 1.47221ptI}}a_{n}\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx^{n}\,\,:\,\,\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{\alpha}\not|\,P.

(61)

Hence,
P
is a polynomial in the variables of the cluster
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx
, which is not divisible by
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{\alpha}
. Here divisibility is intended in the polynomial ring. Up to changing
f
, we can assume that if
n_{\alpha}>0
, then
a_{n}=0
, so that the sum defining
P
runs over
\mathbb{N}^{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}.

We use the convention that, if
\beta\in D
, then
i_{\beta}=\beta
so that
i_{j}
makes sense for any
j\in\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptI
.
The fact that
f
is
X(T)
-homogeneous of degree
\lambda
imposes (using Proposition
8.1.6
) that, for any
n
such that
a_{n}\neq 0

\sum_{j\in\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}n_{j}\varpi_{i_{j}}=\lambda+\varpi_{\alpha}.

(62)

Next, let
x
as in Lemma
8.1.3
. Using Proposition
8.1.6
and Lemma
8.1.4
(recall that
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{\beta}=X_{\beta})
we deduce that

f(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\lambda(h)t^{{}^{-1}}\sum_{k\geq 0}t^{k}f_{k}(y)

for certain
f_{k}\in\mathbb{C}[Y]
(resp.
\mathbb{C}[Y_{\setminus\alpha}]
) if
\alpha\in I
(resp.
\alpha\not\in I
).
For
\beta\in\Delta
, we set
p_{\beta,0}=1
so that, for
\beta\neq\alpha
,

\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{\beta}(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h)=\varpi_{\beta}(h)p_{\beta,0}.

Using the last expression, the fact that
n_{\alpha}=0
if
a_{n}\neq 0
and Proposition
8.1.6
, we get that

f_{0}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.57646pt\kern 1.47221ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}}a_{n}p_{\bullet,0}^{n}\quad\text{where}\quad p_{\bullet,0}^{n}=\prod_{j\in\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}p_{j,0}^{n_{j}}.

But since
f\in{\operatorname{Br}}
,
\iota_{\alpha}^{*}(f)
is regular, hence
f_{0}=0.

Consider the linear map
\widetilde{\pi}:\mathbb{Z}^{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{Z}^{(I\setminus\alpha^{\min})}
defined in the following way.
For
\beta\in D\setminus\{\alpha\}
,
\widetilde{\pi}(e_{\beta})=0
. For
j\in I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}
,
\widetilde{\pi}(e_{j})=e_{j}
and
\widetilde{\pi}(e_{\alpha^{\min}})=\sum_{j<\alpha^{\min}}c_{j}e_{j}
, where the coefficients
c_{j}
are the ones of Lemma
8.1.11
. Since
p_{\beta,0}=1
for
\beta\in D
and because of Lemma
8.1.11
, we have that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}

p_{\bullet,0}^{n}=p_{\bullet,0}^{\widetilde{\pi}(n)}.

In particular

f_{0}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}}}b_{m}p_{\bullet,0}^{m}\quad\text{where}\quad b_{m}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.57646pt\kern 1.47221ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}}\,:\,\widetilde{\pi}(n)=m}a_{n}.

By Lemma
8.1.12
, the functions
p_{j,0}
with
j\in I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}
are algebraically independent.
Since
f_{0}=0
, then for any
m\in\mathbb{N}^{I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}}
,
b_{m}=0
. We claim that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}^{I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}}
, there exists at most one
n\in\mathbb{N}^{{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}}
such that
a_{n}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{\pi}(n)=m
. This implies at ones that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}}
,
a_{n}=0
, which is a contradiction.

To prove the claim, just notice that any
n
such that
a_{n}\neq 0
satisfies the weight condition (
62
). In particular, consider the linear map
\pi:\mathbb{Z}^{{\upharpoonleft\kern-2.8681pt\kern 1.63885ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}}}{\,\longrightarrow\,}X(T)\times\mathbb{Z}^{I\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}}
defined by
\pi(e_{j})=(\varpi_{i_{j}},\widetilde{\pi}(e_{j}))
. Remember that, for
\beta\in\Delta
,
i_{\beta}=\beta
. If
n
is such that
a_{n}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{\pi}(n)=m
, then
\pi(n)=(\lambda+\varpi_{\alpha},m)
. But the map
\pi
is injective. To prove this, consider
\pi
as a matrix with columns indexed by
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptI\setminus\{\alpha\}
and rows indexed by
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptI\setminus\{\alpha^{\min}\}.
Recall that
X(T)
is identified with
\mathbb{Z}^{D}
by means of the fundamental weights. Enumerate the roots of
\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}
from
1
to
r-1
. Then order the columns of the matrix representing
\pi
accordingly to the order

\beta_{1}<\dots<\beta_{r-1}<1<\dots<l

and the rows according to the order

\beta_{1}<\dots<\beta_{r-1}<1<\dots<\alpha^{\min}-1<\alpha<\alpha^{\min}+1<\dots<l.

Then, the matrix representing
\pi
is upper triangular with ones on the diagonal. In particular
\pi
is invertible.
∎

8.2
The product case: tensor product

To simplify the notation, in this section we switch the role of
G
and
{\widehat{G}}
. In particular,
G
is a subgroup of
{\widehat{G}}
. We assume that
G
is semisimple, simply connected and we consider
G
as a subgroup of
{\widehat{G}}=G\times G
by the diagonal embedding. This is the simplest case of Subsection
7.4.2
: when
H=G
. In this section we want to prove the following

Theorem 8.2.1
.

For any
\mathbf{i}\in R(w_{0})
, there’s equality in Theorem
7.4.5
. That is:

\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D})={\operatorname{Br}}(G\times G,G).

Corollary 8.2.2
.

If
G
is simply laced and
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in R(w_{0})
, then
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D}\simeq\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{D}
.

Proof.

Since
G
is simply laced, then
t_{\mathbf{i}}\simeq t_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
. Then
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}}^{D}\simeq\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptt_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{D}
by Theorem
8.2.1
, Corollary
4.0.11
and Lemma
3.0.8
.
∎

Here we chose a maximal torus
T
contained in a Borel
B
, of
G
, and we consider
{\widehat{T}}=T\times T
and
{\widehat{B}}=B\times B
. Because of Remark
7.4.2
, we make no difference between
z=(w_{0},e)
and
w_{0}
. Moreover, we identify
{\widehat{U}}(z)
and
U=U(w_{0})
in the obvious way. Recall that, for any
\mathbf{i}\in R(w_{0})=R(z)
, the seed
t_{\mathbf{i}}
gives a cluster structure to
Y=U
. From now on, we fix
\mathbf{i}=(i_{l},\dots,i_{1})\in R(w_{0})
and set
t=t_{\mathbf{i}}
. Here,
D=D_{l}\sqcup D_{r}
is the disjoint union of two copies of
\Delta
, namely: the
left copy
is
D_{l}=\{\alpha_{l}\,:\,\alpha\in\Delta\}
and the
right copy
is
D_{r}=\{\alpha_{r}\,:\,\alpha\in\Delta\}
. For
\alpha\in\Delta
(see Remark
5.4.8
)

X_{\alpha_{l}}=\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}\otimes 1\quad X_{\alpha_{r}}=1\otimes\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{e,e}.

(63)

We denote
{\operatorname{Br}}={\operatorname{Br}}(G\times G,G)
. Note that
X_{\alpha_{l}}\in{\operatorname{Br}}_{(\varpi_{\alpha},0),\varpi_{\alpha}}
and
X_{\alpha_{r}}\in{\operatorname{Br}}_{(0,\varpi_{\alpha}),\varpi_{\alpha}}
.
The map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}T\times T\times U&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
(h_{l},h_{r},u)&\longmapsto&(h_{l}u,h_{r})\end{array}

induces the
T\times T
-equivariant open embedding
\phi:T\times T\times Y{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}:={\mathfrak{X}}(G\times G,G)
. We have a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{-}\times U^{-}\times T\times T\times U\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times T\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(64)

where the leftmost vertical map is defined by
(u^{-}_{l},u^{-}_{r},h_{l},h_{r},u_{l},u_{r})\longmapsto(h_{l},h_{r},u_{l}u_{r}{{}^{-1}})
,
\pi
is the natural projection and
\psi
is defined by
(u^{-}_{l},u^{-}_{r},h_{l},h_{r},u_{l},u_{r})\longmapsto(u^{-}_{l}h_{l}u_{l}\,,\,u^{-}_{r}h_{r}u_{r})
. By Lemma
8.0.1
, for any
\alpha\in\Delta
, computing the valuation
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}
(resp.
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
) of a rational function on
X
is the same as computing its valuation along the divisor
\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}\times G
(resp.
G\times\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
).

Lemma 8.2.3
.

For
\alpha\in\Delta
, let

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\iota_{\alpha}:T\times T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Y&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
x=(h_{l},h_{r},t,y)&\longmapsto&\iota_{\alpha}(x)=(h_{l}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y,\,\,h_{r})\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{r c l}j_{\alpha}:T\times T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Y&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
x=(h_{l},h_{r},t,y)&\longmapsto&j_{\alpha}(x)=(h_{l}yx_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}}),\,\,h_{r}\alpha^{\vee}(t)).\end{array}

Then, for any
f\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})
,

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}(\iota_{\alpha}^{*}(f))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}(j_{\alpha}^{*}(f)).

Proof.

First, note that the maps obtained by composing
\iota_{\alpha}
and
j_{\alpha}
with the natural projection
G\times G{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\mathfrak{X}}
are dominant. In fact, at
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
fixed, both maps are obtained from
\phi
by an appropriate "multiplication twist" by
\alpha^{\vee}(t)
and
x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})
, so they’re dominant at
t
fixed. Hence
\iota_{\alpha}^{*}
and
j_{\alpha}^{*}
make sense. Since
f
is generically defined on
{\mathfrak{X}}
, we make the assumption, throughout the proof, that the points on which we evaluate
f
are sufficiently generic so that the evaluation makes sense.

We start from the "left side", so we prove the statement about
\iota_{\alpha}
. First, we prove that the map
\phi_{\alpha}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{r c l}U^{-}\times U^{-}\times T\times T\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
x=(u^{-}_{l},u^{-}_{r},h_{l},h_{r},t,r,u,\widetilde{u})&\longmapsto&\phi_{\alpha}(x)=(u_{l}^{-}h_{l}\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)u\widetilde{u},\,\,u_{r}^{-}h_{r}x_{\alpha}(r)\widetilde{u})\end{array}

is an open embedding whose image intersects
\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}\times G
. Note that, for
x
as in the definition of
\phi_{\alpha}
, we have that
\phi_{\alpha}(x)\in\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}\times G
if and only if
t=0
and, by (
31
),
\phi_{\alpha}(x)\in G_{0}\times G_{0}
otherwise.

The map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}:U^{-}\times U^{-}\times T\times T\times U\times U&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
(u^{-}_{l},u^{-}_{r},h_{l},h_{r},u_{l},u_{r})&\longmapsto&(u_{l}^{-}h_{l}u_{l}\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1},\,\,u_{r}^{-}h_{r}u_{r})\end{array}

is clearly an open embedding. Moreover, the map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\mathbb{C}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&U\\
(t,u)&\longmapsto&x_{\alpha}(t)u\end{array}

is an isomorphism and conjugation by
\overline{s}_{\alpha}
is an automorphism of
U_{\setminus\alpha}
. Furthermore, the map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}U_{\setminus\alpha}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&U_{\setminus\alpha}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}\\
u,\widetilde{u}&\longmapsto&u\widetilde{u},\widetilde{u}\end{array}

is an isomorphism. In particular, looking in the definition of
\widetilde{\phi}_{\alpha}
, we see that we can write uniquely
(u_{l}\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1},u_{r})=(x_{\alpha}(-t)\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}u\widetilde{u}\,,\,x_{\alpha}(r)\widetilde{u})
, with
t,r\in\mathbb{C}
and
u,\widetilde{u}\in U_{\setminus\alpha}
. Using that

x_{\alpha}(-t)\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}=\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)

we deduce that
\phi_{\alpha}
is an open embedding.
Now, let
x
as in the definition of
\phi_{\alpha}
, such that
t\neq 0
. Then, by the invariance properties of
f
and (
31
), we have that

f(\phi_{\alpha}(x))=f(h_{l}\overline{s}_{\alpha}^{-1}x_{-\alpha}(t)u,\,\,h_{r}x_{\alpha}(r))=f(h_{l}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})u,\,\,h_{r}x_{\alpha}(r)).

In particular, it’s clear that if

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha}:T\times T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
(h_{l},h_{r},t,r,u)&\longmapsto&(h_{l}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})u,\,\,h_{r}x_{\alpha}(r))\end{array}

then

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}\bigl(\phi_{\alpha}^{*}(f)\bigr)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}\bigl(\widetilde{\iota}^{*}_{\alpha}(f)\bigr).

But

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\mathbb{C}\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&Y\\
(r,u)&\longmapsto&ux_{\alpha}(-r)\end{array}

(65)

is an isomorphism and, since
f
is invariant by the right action of diagonal multiplication by
U
, we have that

f(\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha}(x))=f\biggl((h_{l}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})ux_{\alpha}(-r),h_{r})\biggr).

In particular, under the identification induced by (
65
), we have that

\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha}^{*}(f)=\iota_{\alpha}^{*}(f).

We conclude thanks to the following very simple statement.

Lemma 8.2.4
.

Let
\psi:Z{\,\longrightarrow\,}W
be an isomorphism of normal, complex, algebraic varieties and
\phi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Z{\,\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{C}^{*}\times W
be defined by
(t,z)\longmapsto(t,\psi(z))
. Then, for any
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times W)
,

\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}\phi^{*}(f).

Next, we pass to the "right side", that is to
j_{\alpha}
. The argument is very similar, we sketch through it. First, one proves that the map

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\psi_{\alpha}:U^{-}\times U^{-}\times T\times T\times\mathbb{C}\times Y\times U_{\setminus\alpha}&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
x=(u^{-}_{l},u^{-}_{r},h_{l},h_{r},t,y,u)&\longmapsto&\psi_{\alpha}(x)=(u_{l}^{-}h_{l}yu,\,\,u_{r}^{-}h_{r}x_{\alpha}(t)\overline{s}_{\alpha}u)\end{array}

is an open embedding. Moreover,
\psi_{\alpha}(x)\in G\times\overline{B^{-}s_{\alpha}B}
if
t=0
and
\psi_{\alpha}(x)\in G_{0}\times G_{0}
otherwise. But then, for
t\neq 0
we compute that

f(\psi_{\alpha}(x))=f\biggl((h_{l}y,\,\,h_{r}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}})\biggr).

In particular, considering

\begin{array}[]{r c l}\widetilde{j}_{\alpha}:T\times T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Y&{\,\longrightarrow\,}&G\times G\\
(h_{l},h_{r},t,y)&\longmapsto&(h_{l}y,\,\,h_{r}\alpha^{\vee}(t)x_{\alpha}(-t{{}^{-1}}))\end{array}

we clearly have that

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}\psi_{\alpha}^{*}(f)=\mathcal{V}_{\{t=0\}}\widetilde{j_{\alpha}}^{*}(f).

But then, by invariance of
f
, we see that
\widetilde{j_{\alpha}}^{*}(f)=j^{*}_{\alpha}(f).

∎

Then, we make this important but very easy calculation.

Lemma 8.2.5
.

Let
(\lambda,\mu)\in X(T)\times X(T)=X(T\times T)
,
f\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{(\lambda,\mu),\bullet}
and
x
generic as in the notation of Lemma
8.2.3
. Then, for any
\alpha\in\Delta
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}f(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\lambda(h_{l})\mu(h_{r})t^{\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}f((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y,e))&\text{and}\\[3.00003pt]
f(j_{\alpha}(x))=\lambda(h_{l})\mu(h_{r})t^{\langle\mu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}f((yx_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}}),\,\,e)).\end{array}

Moreover, for any
\beta\in\Delta

\begin{array}[]{ll}X_{\beta_{l}}(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h_{l})t^{\langle\varpi_{\beta},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}&X_{\beta_{r}}(\iota_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h_{r})\\
X_{\beta_{l}}(j_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h_{l})&X_{\beta_{r}}(j_{\alpha}(x))=\varpi_{\beta}(h_{r})t^{\langle\varpi_{\beta},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}\end{array}

Remark 8.2.6
.

By little abuse,
\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{(\lambda,\mu),\bullet}
denotes the set of
T\times T
semi-invariant rational functions of weight
(\lambda,\mu).

Proof.

The proof is trivial.
∎

Recall that, an element
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, is identified with the character

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}(\mu_{\alpha_{l}}\varpi_{\alpha},\mu_{\alpha_{r}}\varpi_{\alpha})\in X(T\times T).

For any
\alpha\in\Delta
and
v,w\in W
, we denote by
D^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
the restriction of
\Delta^{\varpi_{\alpha}}_{v,w}
to
Y=U
.
Here, we describe explicitly the cluster variables
\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx
and how they behave in the charts given by
\iota_{\alpha}
and
j_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 8.2.7
.

For any
k\in[l]
and
\alpha\in\Delta

\nu_{\alpha_{l},k}=\delta_{i_{k},\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{\alpha_{r},k}=\max\{0,\langle-z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\}.

Moreover,

\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}\bigl(\iota_{\alpha}(x)\bigr)=\nu_{\bullet,k}\bigl((h_{l},h_{r})\bigr)\biggl(p_{k,0}(y)+tp_{k,1}(y)\biggr)\quad\text{where}\quad p_{k,0}=D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}

and

\upharpoonleft\kern-3.50006ptx_{k}\bigl(j_{\alpha}(x)\bigr)=\nu_{\bullet,k}\bigl((h_{l},h_{r})\bigr)\biggl(\sum_{n=0}^{\nu_{\alpha_{r},k}}t^{n}q_{k,n}(y)\biggr)\quad\text{where}\quad q_{k,0}=\begin{cases}D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}&\text{if}\quad(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\\
D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,s_{\alpha}z_{\leq k}^{-1}}&\text{if}\quad(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Note that
1\otimes x_{k}\in\mathbb{C}({\mathfrak{X}})_{(0,0),\bullet}
. In particular, by Lemma
8.2.5
we have that

1\otimes x_{k}\bigl(\iota_{\alpha}(x)\bigr)=1\otimes x_{k}\bigl((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y,e)\bigr).

But (see diagram (
64
))

\phi\biggl((e,e,x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)\biggr)=\pi\biggl((x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y,e)\biggr).

Then, by the definition of
1\otimes x_{k}
, we have that

1\otimes x_{k}\bigl(\iota_{\alpha}(x)\bigr)=x_{k}\bigl(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y\bigr).

Now we apply Lemma
5.4.7
.

If
i_{k}\neq\alpha
, then

x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)=D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{e,z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y)=D^{\varpi_{i_{k}}}_{s_{\alpha},z_{\leq k}^{-1}}(y)=p_{k,0}(y).

(66)

The central equality follows from Lemma
5.2.1
. In particular, by Lemma
8.2.3
, we deduce that
\nu_{\alpha_{l},k}=0.

If
i_{k}=\alpha
, then

x_{k}(x_{\alpha}(t{{}^{-1}})y)=p_{k,1}(y)+t{{}^{-1}}p_{k,0}(y)

(67)

where
p_{k,0}
is as described in the statement and
p_{k,1}
can be deduced from Lemma
5.4.7
. By Lemma
5.4.1
,
p_{k,0}
doesn’t vanish on
Y
. Hence, form Lemma
8.2.3
, we deduce that
\nu_{\alpha_{l},k}=\delta_{i_{k},\alpha}
.

We switch to
j_{\alpha}
. The argument is very similar. We set
N_{k}(\alpha)=\max\{0,\langle-z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\}
.
First, observe that if
(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
, then
N_{k}(\alpha)=0
and

1\otimes x_{k}(j_{\alpha}(x))=D^{\varpi_{i,k}}_{e,z_{\leq k^{-1}}}(y)

(68)

by Lemmas
8.2.5
and
5.4.3
. If
(z_{\leq k})\alpha\in\Phi^{-}
, then
N_{k}(\alpha)=\langle-z_{\leq k}^{-1}\varpi_{i_{k}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle.
Again, Lemmas
8.2.5
and
5.4.3
imply that

1\otimes x_{k}(j_{\alpha}(x))=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{k}(\alpha)}t^{-n}Q_{(N_{k}(\alpha)-n)}(y).

(69)

Then, we set
q_{k,m}=Q_{m}
. By Lemma
5.4.3
,
q_{k,0}
